First-person commentary

The first thing I notice about the page is the amount of space it gives to blue sky before asking me to read tens of thousands of words. In the photograph, I can take in the layout almost at once. Sky above, tawny grass below, darker woods between, and water partly hidden inside those woods. The transcript changes what it means to linger on that view. Once I have read about the shadow, the bare steps, the flowers, and the search for a tree on a far crest, I go back to the photograph looking for different kinds of things. I spend more time with the flattened grass at the lower left and the small yellow flowers. The picture stays the same, but the text gives me several possible next looks.

The videos as described in the transcript also make me think about how little time a camera has to spend with a detail for that detail to reorganize a place. The cyclist is visible at a useful scale for scarcely two seconds. In that interval, the lower trail becomes a route used by someone moving at a different speed and elevation. The bare feet supply almost the reverse kind of information. They bring the route down to the small uneven area where a step will land. I find the still of the stones particularly effective after reading that passage. The nearest boulder is cut off at the bottom, which places it almost underneath the viewer. The rocks then draw my eye uphill, but the photograph gives me time to look at the tan face of the slab before following them.

The green trough makes me reconsider the colors in the opening photograph. The earlier reply entertains the mat as a possible referent for the filename. In the monologue, karbytes uses the Big Green Thing as a name for a wider landscape that includes Horsey Hill. I read the trough as a small echo of that larger terrain. The opening photograph has dark tree-covered shapes surrounded in places by water. The trough description has green shapes separated by little dark channels and holes. I can imagine my eye following both arrangements in a similar way, even though one is a valley view and the other fits inside a concrete rectangle. The close view of the mat also makes me appreciate how sparingly green is distributed in much of the October material. At a distance it gathers in wooded folds. Nearby it can be a shrub, a few leaves among the dried stems, or an unexpectedly vivid surface in a basin.

The tiny possible summit tree holds my attention for a different reason. Karbytes recognizes it by attending to something closer and easier to place, the row of eucalyptus trees. I find that much more interesting than a crisp picture of an isolated tree would have been. The proposed identity depends on the order in which features appear from this position. Shift the viewpoint and the useful alignment might change. I also like that the speech keeps the qualification. The thought is worth recording while it is still taking shape. It leaves me wondering how much of a familiar view consists of features someone has seen many times and only once thought to connect.

I see a related kind of comparison in the green-light report. The steady entrance helps make sense of the green patch’s disappearance. With the two areas in frame, I can understand why the analyst compares them instead of treating the image as one overall brightness value. What interests me most is the change in available detail. A moment earlier, leaves and horizontal masonry courses can be distinguished. A moment later, I would have to remember those details to know what occupies the darkness. The p8 still preserves the earlier appearance well enough to give that memory a shape. The little video gives it a before and after. And at the scale of the lights seen from Horsey Hill, a changing brightness and a fixed position can both be true of the same place.

The shower passage gives me a different limit to think about. A bright point across the lake might call up warm water for the person who knows the campground. The water temperature is available to that person through a hand held in the spray. On the page, I have a description of the visible gesture and a qualified spoken judgment. The words “kind of warm” matter to me because they retain the modest scale of the discovery. Something was tried and found adequate. The later description of a cheap vacation gives that modest fact a much larger reach. A place nearby becomes more usable because karbytes now knows what happens after pressing one particular button.

I find the file closure equally practical, though it works in the other direction. A line added on September 21 says it is expanding KARBYTES by being added. The September 29 line repeats that operation. The October line also expands the file, and what it adds is a decision to stop expanding it. I like the specificity of this ending. A reader can see the two earlier acts and the exact sentence that ends the routine. The outdoor reminder gives the decision a setting and a reason that the closed file itself has little room to carry. Together they make me wonder which acts of maintenance are worth doing on a schedule, which can be replaced by occasional records, and how an archive can preserve a completed practice without asking its author to reenact it.

The correction from the shower clip to the exterior clip is one of the page’s smallest exchanges, and I find it important to how the rest reads. A single suffix determines whether I encounter a hand testing water or green foliage disappearing into darkness. The editor repairs the reference that readers will use, while the dialogue retains the fact that someone had to recognize the mismatch. The conflicting April dates have a similar effect on my reading. The page lets me follow a strong relative sequence from the photographs to the exterior video to the shower, while leaving the difference between the filename date and the recorded clocks visible. I can be interested in the timing without having to make every label tell the same story.

When I return to the opening photograph after the campground material, I am inclined to imagine the opposite transition from the one at the green-lit wall. In daylight I can separate individual trees, see the bends of the water, and follow the edge of the grassy slope. Toward night, those shapes would gradually become harder to read, while a few deliberately placed lights would acquire more importance. A distant viewer familiar with the area might still have the path, the entrance, and the warm shower in mind. My eye ends up back at the photograph’s darker band of trees, considering how much of a place can be present to someone who sees only a few points of light.

The first use of karbytes denotes the person who authored the website. The later use denotes the intellectual property encompassing the two sites.

That upper passage says that karbytes declares each file in the two-site collection open source and public domain as soon as it is published on the public web. It also describes a practice of routinely saving files to the Wayback Machine during karbytes’ functional lifespan. The stated aim is to increase the chance that the intended final versions will remain publicly accessible and fixed in their archived form. The passage distinguishes a publication policy from the work of preservation. A file is declared available for public use when it is published. Making and maintaining archival copies is how the author tries to give that availability a longer life.

A band of white space separates the upper passage from a nine-line footnote introduced by a parenthesized asterisk. The word “genome” has an orange background. The last sentence begins partway through the seventh line of the footnote. An underline carries it across the last three lines, through the closing parenthesis. It presumes exactly one such organism and adds that the organism is also named karbytes. The underlining gives the sentence a visual prominence separate from the colored publication declaration above it. The PNG therefore preserves the original claim as a fixed excerpt, while the live source and the page displaying the excerpt both give the reader the qualification in text.

The two sites and the two genome references

The identity page calls its definition a combination of karbytes’ genome and the assumption that only karbytes originally authored the two websites. It labels Karlina Object as website_0 and Karbytes For Life Blog as website_1. The links are set on black, with green text for the first and orange for the second. The qualification about copies of karbytes

The cyan paragraph says the screenshot’s uniqueness presumption could be factually incorrect. It first asks the reader to consider a hypothetical multiverse containing parallel universes that are phenomenally identical. Under that possibility, another universe could contain a counterpart with the same genome, name, websites, and apparent history of authorship. The paragraph then widens the question to all noumena. It proposes that there could always be infinitely many concurrent copies of the same “phenomenally distinct” noumenon, whether or not that noumenon ever has a phenomenal counterpart. The author describes this second setting as even harder to rule out.

Three of the key words in the qualification lead to other definitions in the KARBYTES project. MULTIVERSE describes universes as individual spacetime continua and treats a multiverse as the hypothetical existence of multiple universes, simultaneously or asynchronously. NATURE defines a noumenon in relation to a partial frame of reference. An example has one observer directly registering a phenomenon and another observer for whom that same phenomenon is unobserved. NATURE also proposes indefinitely many distinct noumenal patterns and infinitely many copies of each. NUMBERS describes infinity in terms of limitless copies of one pattern or limitless distinct patterns. These definitions give the qualification a more specific meaning within the project. The possibility at issue is that a pattern distinguishable from other patterns might itself have multiple instances, including instances beyond the reach of a particular observer.

The page thus uses uniqueness in two settings. In the local setting, the genome, names, and original authorship work together to point to karbytes in the history of these two sites. In the wider setting, the document asks whether an indistinguishable individual and an indistinguishable history could occur elsewhere. The highlighted note leaves that possibility open. It does not provide an observed counterpart or establish how many instances there are.

First-person commentary

I read the underlined sentence and the cyan qualification as the two most important marks on the page. The underline tells me exactly where the earlier text put its weight. The cyan paragraph then directs me back to a few words in that sentence, especially “always exactly one.” I appreciate being able to see the claim before seeing it reconsidered. If the July text had simply been replaced, I would have the later position but would lose the experience of watching the scope of the earlier sentence become a problem. Here the image keeps the wording available for inspection. The surrounding prose changes how confidently I can read it.

I find that “exactly one” invites two questions that are easy to merge. Does the description pick out someone? Could it pick out anyone else? In the ordinary history of the sites, the page gives me several ways of finding the person it means. The names, the two publications, and the genetic references point in the same direction. In the imagined case of a duplicated universe, that same richness makes the question sharper. I have to ask whether I am counting the author of these particular publications or counting every counterpart who has the same relation to corresponding publications. A counterpart might stand in precisely the same relation to its own sites as karbytes stands to these. The words “those two websites” do a surprising amount of work once I start asking what belongs inside the count.

The screenshot copies give me a concrete version of that difficulty. I can say that I examined two files and that I examined one image. Both statements fit the evidence. The two downloads have different byte lengths and a different arrangement of compressed data. They produce the same array of pixels, right down to the marks under the last sentence. Which count I use depends on what I am tracking. I can extend that thought to the page’s hypothetical counterparts without assuming that people can be compared as readily as PNGs. The image gives me a finite criterion I can actually check. The thought experiment asks what the criterion would be for declaring two entire lives phenomenally identical, and who could apply it.

I also notice that the definition combines properties that travel in different ways. A file can transmit a name, a genomic description, or the text of a website. A statement about original authorship points me toward an event and the history that followed it. Reading an exact copy can give me excellent access to the words that were published. To connect those words to the act of composing and first publishing them, I look to provenance. That difference helps me understand why a project committed to broad copying would devote a page to identifying its origin. The more freely a work can travel, the more often a reader will encounter it far from the circumstances that produced it. The link between the copy and those circumstances can be worth preserving in its own right.

The use of karbytes for both a person and a collection becomes especially interesting to me in that light. The screenshot changes the color when it changes the referent. I see the name at the start of a sentence as the person capable of deciding what to publish and how to license it. A few words later, I see the same string as the body of material to which that decision applies. One can be encountered through the other. I can meet the archive through a copied file and find a trace of the person in its wording, in its date, or in a distinction as small as a colored link. I wonder how much of the identity a future reader would reconstruct from those details if that reader arrived at a single file, without knowing which site originally held it.

The word “always” gives me a further reason to pause. The screenshot speaks of a functional lifespan in one paragraph and of a unique organism in the next. I can read the latter as a claim about how many individuals have the relevant history. I can also read it as a claim about how many organisms exist at any moment. Those readings put time in different places. An event such as first publishing a site can remain part of someone’s biography after the action is over. Counting an organism at a particular moment asks a different question. The multiverse passage makes that ambiguity more pronounced for me, because its linked definition allows different universes to exist asynchronously. I would want to know what makes two instances concurrent when they belong to separate timelines. The page is brief enough to leave that question genuinely open.

The move from parallel universes to noumena interests me most where the note asks about a pattern that may never have a phenomenal counterpart. The example in NATURE is relatively easy to picture. Someone else can observe what I currently cannot. The stronger possibility is harder to give a shape. If a purported copy never enters any observable relation, what would distinguish a world with one such instance from a world with many? I can understand why the page treats that possibility as difficult to rule out. I would still ask what, within the proposed framework, lets me count two instances separately. The more perfectly they match and the further they are from observation, the more the question turns on whether the framework gives each instance a distinct place, history, or relation.

The routine of saving files to the Wayback Machine brings me back to a scale at which the page can make a concrete choice. The author can publish a document, name its source, preserve a dated view, and let the live text develop. The screenshot is already doing a small version of that work. It carries one configuration of a paragraph into a later context where the paragraph can be considered anew. I like that the old sentence still ends in the same place and the underline still reaches its closing parenthesis. I can see exactly what was preserved, and I can see the new question sitting beside it.

How the archive was prepared

The October 1 journal describes a rehearsal using the hg38 human reference genome. Karbytes reports downloading a compressed reference FASTA of roughly 983.7 megabytes to practice preserving a file of that kind before the personal sequencing result arrived. The journal describes a storage problem during decompression and an experiment with dividing the compressed file into smaller pieces for the Wayback Machine. Karbytes settled on pieces of roughly five megabytes, recombined them, and compared checksums. The rehearsal used a reference sequence. Its relevance to the November archive is the preservation method it tested.

In the October 16 conversation, karbytes reports that the sequencing service had received the sample and was checking it before processing. Much of the discussion concerns what the service might deliver and what would be practical to keep. The three journal files in part 0 have November 6 in their filenames and November 7, 2025 in their authorial headers. The file marked .1 contains the first read from each fragment, and the file marked .2 contains the second read from the other end. Karbytes selected the .1 file as the input for the archive. The journal gives its compressed size as approximately 30.8 gigabytes. The 30-fold designation refers to the sequencing dataset described there. The published conversion takes one of its two mate files as input.

The p1 journal records the conversion. Karbytes used seqtk in FASTA-output mode and passed the resulting stream to gzip. The seqtk documentation describes that operation as a FASTQ-to-FASTA conversion. In this workflow, the identifiers and nucleotide sequences are carried into FASTA, while the per-base quality scores belong to the FASTQ input. Karbytes reports that the conversion took about two hours and produced a gzip-compressed FASTA of approximately 15.7 gigabytes. The journal’s account explains why the archive is most precisely described as a read-level FASTA from a whole-genome sequencing run.

The same journal describes a second compression experiment using NAF, the Nucleotide Archival Format. Karbytes installed the NAF tools, decompressed the gzip stream into the NAF encoder, and obtained a file of approximately 12.8 gigabytes. That was smaller than the gzip copy and still exceeded the space available for this purpose on the karbytes_origins M-DISC. The account then returns to the gzip version and the approach tested in October. The distributed pieces are fragments of the gzip-compressed FASTA.

The p2 journal documents the split, the join, and the check. Karbytes renamed the compressed file and used the Unix split command with a size of 5M and four-digit decimal suffixes. In that command, 5M means five mebibytes, or 5,242,880 bytes. The sequence begins at chunk_0000 and ends at chunk_2997. The four-digit padding keeps ordinary filename sorting aligned with numeric order. Reassembly consists of concatenating the pieces in that order to restore the gzip file, which can then be opened as compressed FASTA. The split occurs at byte offsets in the compressed stream. The part numbers describe storage divisions, with the biological sequence represented inside the reconstructed file.

Karbytes reports computing SHA-256 for the original gzip file and again for the file produced by concatenation. The journal gives the same digest for both.

5898744ef0b3c7f41e4f4cf4f5078acfde93a6dfea1b44529e8f81f4ef676331

That is the author’s reported check that the split and local reassembly recovered the original compressed file. The check applies to the bytes of that gzip file, which is already the result of the earlier conversion from FASTQ.

MICRO_DIRECTORY_DEFINITION

The page defines the micro-directory as a unit of KARBYTES that can be browsed on WordPress and recovered through GitHub. Each micro-directory has one designated entry in a larger index and one corresponding public repository. The directory page gives readers a way to see the repository as a collection of individually addressable files. The definition then explains where the directory page’s own source can be kept, how the micro-directories fit together, and why reconstructing the larger collection also involves a separate preservation index. It closes by distinguishing karbytes, the person who created the collection, from KARBYTES, the collection itself.

The page appears on KARBYTES_FOR_LIFE_BLOG, under the site tagline “A Cyberspace Journey.” The title is repeated in bold at the head of the article. A yellow-highlighted opening paragraph defines the term specifically within KARBYTES, the author’s collection of public-domain, web-hosted files. It describes karbytes as the self-named “human-to-cybernetic information processing agent” who created the collection. Links within that opening lead to the project’s explanations of public domain, agency, species identification, and the creator’s unique identity. An italicized notice dated May 30, 2026 directs readers to a preserved version of the definition from September 12, 2025.

Horizontal rules divide the four characteristics, the additional notes, and the footer. Each characteristic has a bold label numbered from zero through three. Underlining emphasizes the two ways a repository can be organized, the statement about reconstructing the full collection, and the claim about saving its files to the Wayback Machine. The two naming statements near the end are highlighted cyan. Throughout the page, some links use orange or green lettering against black, while several general reference links use black against orange. The color and emphasis make the document read partly like a specification and partly like an annotated map of the archive.

The first characteristic. One place in the macro directory

CHARACTERISTIC_0 gives each micro-directory exactly one reference on the page named RAW_GITHUB_FILES_MACRO_DIRECTORY. The macro directory is itself one of the top-level pages accessible from the Karbytes For Life Blog home page. The result is a navigational chain. The home page leads to the macro directory, the macro directory leads to the individual micro-directory, and the micro-directory leads to its repository and raw files.

The exactly-one rule is about the micro-directory’s entry in that particular index. The same directory can also appear in related pages and in the separate preservation index. In the copy of the macro directory I examined, the primary entries are numbered consecutively from 0 through 163. Each has one distinct directory destination. Entry 60 also carries a small asterisk that leads to a related journal entry, in addition to its directory link. That journal entry concerns videos saved to the Wayback Machine. The macro directory explains its own color convention. Mint-green index numbers mark directories assumed to be finalized, and lavender marks directories expected to receive further updates. The second characteristic. One repository and a readable file list

CHARACTERISTIC_1 pairs each micro-directory with exactly one corresponding public GitHub repository under the account @karlinarayberinger. The page describes two organizing principles for the contents of such a repository. It can preserve source code and media from a chronological phase of karbytes’ life. It can also collect files related by a theme within the larger archive. The first principle gives a period its own container. The second lets material on one subject be gathered even when it has a different relationship to the project’s timeline.

The WordPress directory page gives the reader a link to the corresponding repository near the top. Below it is a list of links to the files held there. The genome archive makes the distinction concrete. Its introductory page presents a single subject, but the data is divided across four repositories, each with its own micro-directory. The first repository carries the preparation and reconstruction journals in addition to its numbered data chunks. The other directories continue the chunk sequence. The directory boundaries help distribute and locate the files. The continuous numbering and the instructions in part 0 explain how the files work together after retrieval.

The third characteristic. Recovering the directory and its source

CHARACTERISTIC_2 says that a micro-directory can be reproduced from the files in its corresponding repository, with an explicit exception for the directory web page itself in some cases. The document accounts for that exception in the next sentence. The source of a micro-directory page can be stored in its own repository or in a newer micro-directory’s repository. Recovering one directory can therefore involve two locations. The associated repository supplies its contents, while a later repository may supply the HTML that presented them.

The older definition gives a specific example. It identifies the 2025 Big Green Thing directory as a case whose own page source is held in KARLINA_OBJECT extension pack 49. I checked the referenced HTML file. It preserves the directory heading, its explanation of batch archiving, the repository reference, and the file list. Its footer gives September 11, 2025 as its last update. The files listed there belong to the Big Green Thing collection, while a copy of the page that organizes those links is stored in another collection. This is the kind of relationship the current definition makes room for.

The word “reproduced” thus applies to two things a reader can distinguish in practice. There are the media and source files being gathered, and there is the directory page that tells the reader how they were grouped and presented. The definition treats the second as material worth preserving in its own right. It allows that material to be deposited later and elsewhere in the archive.

The fourth characteristic. Reconstructing KARBYTES as a whole

CHARACTERISTIC_3 expands from the recovery of a single directory to the recovery of the whole collection. The current wording names two sources. The first is the set of files listed by the micro-directories in RAW_GITHUB_FILES_MACRO_DIRECTORY. The second is the material referenced through the separate GitHub-hosted preservation cluster operated through @karbytessupporter. The cluster begins at karbytes_wayback_machine_saves_precision_batches. The two accounts have distinct roles in this description. The micro-directories’ corresponding public repositories belong to @karlinarayberinger. The external cluster publishes another way to locate and group material for preservation.

The separate cluster makes the file lists smaller and more manageable for Wayback Machine saves. Its landing page describes a maximum of 20 KARBYTES links per sub-index and says that the first batch for a micro-directory begins with the micro-directory page itself. The extensions I examined state a maximum of 21. The first batch for the 2023 summer starter pack contains 19 links, beginning with its WordPress directory and continuing with its repository license and source files. The first genome batch contains 21 links. Its directory page is followed by the license, the three preparation journals, and the first sixteen numbered chunks. The cluster also has forward and backward links between its larger indexes, so a collection can be split into small batches without losing the route between them.

The current definition gives the separate cluster another job. KARBYTES contains pages and media it calls “Easter eggs.” These sit outside the ordinary micro-directory listings and can be reached through subtle links in pages within the collection. The definition identifies WordPress and private GitHub repositories as places where such material is stored. It also says the separate cluster gives each Easter egg an explicit, full-address reference. A piece can therefore be understated or indirect in the experience of browsing the main sites and still have a conspicuous place in an inventory intended for preservation.

The linked M_DISC catalog, mdisc_karbytes2026_4, illustrates the distinction between a published page, its source, and a stored backup. It lists ZIP files, names and sizes, and describes some private-repository archives as potentially containing public KARBYTES material in the form of unlisted WordPress page source and associated media. Two examples are the archives identified as karbytes_basement_53 and karbytes_basement_54, listed at 147.1 and 31.1 megabytes. The catalog makes those backups visible as inventory entries. The definition explains how separately listed references help account for their corresponding published material.

The page says karbytes created the external cluster to make saving each KARBYTES file to the Wayback Machine easier. It also states that karbytes has verified every file as saveable there. I read that as the author’s claim about what can be captured. My checks covered the published indexes, representative batches, and the linked catalog. The page’s statement about saveability concerns a different question from whether a given archived capture exists for every file at a particular date.

What changed from the September 2025 definition

The preserved September 12, 2025 version already has the same basic structure. It defines the micro-directory as a partition of Karbytes For Life Blog, gives it one entry in the macro directory and one corresponding public GitHub repository, and recognizes that its own page source can be kept elsewhere. It is more procedural in one place. It explains that the repository’s green Code button provides a ZIP of the files and gives the Big Green Thing page as an example of source stored in a later repository.

Its fourth characteristic initially describes the macro directory as holding almost all the public source and media for Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog. An attached qualification already mentions at least one hidden directory whose material can be reached by following a finite sequence of public links from the blog’s home page. It also already points to the precision-batch cluster as an unabridged list. The idea of a second route into the archive was present in September.

The May revision makes that route part of the main definition. It expressly describes reconstruction as drawing on both the macro-directory listings and the separate cluster. It names Easter eggs as a class of material, identifies WordPress and private repositories as storage locations, and points to an optical-disc catalog as an example of where the private repository names and sizes are documented. It also gives a fuller description of the ordinary micro-directory page, including its visible raw-file addresses and the chronological or thematic character of the associated repository. The revision puts both ordinary browsing and preservation in the body of the specification.

The older version is itself presented as copied content inside a later page. Its interior preserves the September 12, 2025 date, followed by an end-of-copy marker and the wrapper’s May 30, 2026 footer. The current page points back to that wrapper. The pair gives readers a way to compare versions while keeping the dates of the original text and its later presentation distinct.

The naming distinction

The additional notes establish a convention for relatively modern files. The lowercase name karbytes identifies the creator, also named Kar Beringer. The uppercase name KARBYTES identifies the allocation of cyberspace that the creator assembled. The first statement points to karbytes_unique_identity for the fuller account of the creator’s identity, which includes genomic references and the stated claim of original authorship. The second points to KARBYTES_CODE_POSSIBLY_STOPS_GROWING for a fuller description of the collection and its publication and preservation boundaries. It names Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog as the two websites encompassed by that account.

The distinction is particularly visible when the May page is placed beside the September version. The older version uses lowercase karbytes for the body of intellectual property. The newer page expressly gives lowercase and uppercase separate referents. Its wording “relatively modern” leaves room for earlier documents to retain the terminology of their own period. The linked species-identification page likewise describes changes in karbytes’ self-description and the decision to leave an earlier usage in place as a record of its time. The naming notes at the end of the definition tell a reader how to interpret the current convention when moving among documents written at different dates.

First-person commentary

I find the definition most interesting where it makes a directory into an object that can itself be preserved. When I open an ordinary file list, I tend to think of it as something a host generates from whatever happens to be in a folder at the time. Here the list is published as a page with its own wording, order, color, and provenance. There is a version of that page worth keeping, even if the repository can still tell me which files it holds. The preserved Big Green Thing directory makes that distinction tangible. The repository can supply the files, and a later extension pack can tell me how those files were offered to readers at a particular moment.

The one-link rule gives me a different kind of clarity. I can start at the macro directory and know where the primary entry for a collection sits. From there, I can find that same collection again through a topical index or a small preservation batch. The repetition makes sense to me because each route starts from a different need. I would start with karbytes_full_genome if my interest were the sequencing archive. I would start with the macro directory if I were trying to place its repositories among the rest of KARBYTES. I would start with the precision batches if I were trying to track what had been sent for archiving in manageable groups. The same directory can have one designated position and several useful approaches.

I also keep thinking about the difference between a title and a full raw-file address. A title can tell me what a page calls a photograph or program. The exposed address also tells me where the project put it and under what filename. In a long list, that is more text than I need for casual reading. When I am trying to trace a file, the extra text is the part I want. I can imagine opening a saved copy of a directory years later, finding that one link no longer resolves as it once did, and still learning something from the preserved path. The list retains an account of where the file belonged even when my access to that location has changed.

The newer-repository exception makes me wonder where I would draw the boundary of a complete copy. I might recover every media file in an older repository and still be missing the way those files were arranged for the reader. When I recover the directory HTML from a later pack, I gain back the grouping, the explanatory paragraphs, and the outgoing references. I can now recover more of the experience of navigating the collection. There is a further question about the surrounding site layout, embedded material, and the dates at which different copies were captured. I find it useful that the definition starts with something concrete I can check, the source file, while leaving room to ask which other aspects of presentation belong to the record.

The Easter eggs interest me because the page deliberately gives them two kinds of visibility. In one setting I might arrive at one by noticing a small link while reading another page. In the preservation list I can approach it as a named destination I am deliberately looking for. I enjoy the difference between discovering a page and being able to account for it. If a future reader had only the complete index, they could reach the file while missing the small decision about where the original link was placed. If they had only a saved copy of the surrounding page, they might retain the cue and never follow it. Preserving both paths keeps more of the experience available.

I read the archive-saving claim in a similarly practical way. A file can be eligible to save, a batch can be submitted, a capture can exist, and that capture can later yield the intended bytes. Each is a recognizable event or condition. The smaller batches make me think about the interval between them. With a very large page, I may know all the intended targets while finding it difficult to say what one run reached. A batch small enough to read from top to bottom lets me keep the page and its targets in mind together. The genome example gives me a particularly clear version of that benefit. I can see the instructions, license, and first sixteen chunks together, then imagine moving to the next group with an identifiable place to resume.

The choice to preserve the older definition on its own page seems to enact the distinction the newer one makes between content and provenance. I can see that the earlier text already knew about the hidden material and the external list. The revision changes where that knowledge sits in the argument. What had been attached to the end of an otherwise repository-centered account becomes part of the stated reconstruction method. That is a small editorial move with real consequences for how I read the archive. I might have treated the second list as a convenience if I stopped at the older main paragraph. The current definition makes it part of the account I need to follow.

Finally, I find myself reading the lowercase and uppercase names the way I read the two sorts of directory links. A small visible difference tells me what kind of thing I am addressing. When the page says karbytes created and verified the collection, I read about a person taking responsibility for a series of acts. When it says KARBYTES can be reproduced, I read about files, locations, and relations someone else can follow. The older wording preserves a period when the same spelling carried both jobs. I am glad I can still see that usage beside the newer convention. It leaves me with a concrete example of what the archive holds beyond source files and media. It can preserve a change in how its author named the person doing the work and the material the work left behind.

The page places that rule beside a different prospect. Even if karbytes stops editing, the collection may or may not survive in the exact configuration in which karbytes last arranged it. The decision about when to call the work finished and the problem of keeping it available are both part of the protocol.

The possibility of stopping

The longest paragraph holds three aspirations together. The paragraph also allows for two things that could interrupt that ideal. Karbytes may cease updating it, and the collection may cease to exist in its most recently arranged configuration. The first concerns the creator’s activity. The second concerns what happens to the files and their arrangement. Either can change independently of the other.

The policy responds to that possibility by giving inactivity a defined meaning. When no changes have been made to any of the constituent files for ten years or longer, the archive may be assumed permanently closed to further changes and is thereby declared immutable according to its author’s policy. The condition applies to the collection as a whole. A date on one page describes the history of that page. Applying the rule would require establishing a continuous interval of inactivity across the collection the protocol defines. The document gives the threshold and the interpretation, leaving the start date to the history of the files.

The final sentence says that only karbytes is generally assumed to implement changes to the collection. This assigns changes to the archive to its author in the ordinary reading of the record. First-person commentary

A decade is long enough that I would be wary of treating my own failure to see an update as evidence that there had been none. They can give a later reader several records to compare. Even then, I imagine there could be questions about what kind of change mattered. The original archive could become settled while the record of its circulation continued to grow.

KARBYTES_CONTENT_UPDATING_RULES

KARBYTES_CONTENT_UPDATING_RULES sets two conditions for the continued editing of KARBYTES. CMR0 says that successive updates occur within a decade of one another and that, once more than ten Earth years have elapsed since the last update, readers may assume the collection is permanently closed. CMR1 names Kar Beringer, who uses the name karbytes, as the sole person authorized to update it. The two rules address different parts of the same question. One establishes when the original collection can still be understood as open to revision. The other establishes whose revisions count as acts of its authorized editor.

The page and its source

The page appears on KARBYTES_FOR_LIFE_BLOG, under the tagline “A Cyberspace Journey.” Its uppercase title appears as the page heading, again in bold at the start of the article, and once more inside the reproduced journal text. It locates that pack within the blog’s RAW_GITHUB_FILES_MACRO_DIRECTORY and explains that portions of the copied plain text were converted to hypertext. Below the note, an italic sentence presents this page as an extension of KARBYTES_RULES_FOR_ITSELF.

The reproduced text sits in a monospace block. It opens with a source-code-style comment that gives the filename, file type, date, author, and license. The filename contains April 4, 2026, while the date in the header is April 5, 2026. The author is identified as karbytes and the license as PUBLIC_DOMAIN. The repeated document title is followed by three italic definitions. Rows of three asterisks separate the definitions, the rules, and the final cross-reference. The opening sentence of each rule is bold blue. Its explanation follows in regular monospace type. Orange or green lettering on black marks the internal links, including links from particular words within the rules. The effect is a compact specification whose definitions and antecedents remain available to the reader.

I compared the central block with the 1,230-byte plain-text journal. All eighteen nonblank lines agree after accounting for whitespace and HTML line breaks. The archived HTML also preserves the surrounding presentation and references.

The terms the rules use

The first definition expands CMR as Content Management Rule. It uses CMR[N] to represent a rule with a nonnegative numerical index and allows for a rule set whose upper index is at least N. The document supplies CMR0 and CMR1. The notation gives the two rules stable names while leaving room for additional numbered rules.

The second definition gives uppercase KARBYTES a particular scope. It is the public, web-hosted file collection encompassed by Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog. The word KARBYTES links to KARBYTES_CODE_POSSIBLY_STOPS_GROWING, the earlier protocol that describes in more detail how pages, embedded files, and qualifying repository files belong to the collection. Both sites are named in the April document. Its rules concern the collection across them.

The third definition reserves lowercase karbytes for the single original author and single original proprietor of KARBYTES. It points to karbytes_unique_identity for the fuller description of that person. The capitalization makes a substantive difference throughout the page. The uppercase word identifies the body of work that can be updated. The lowercase word identifies the person whose decision governs those updates.

CMR0 and the interval between changes

CMR0 opens with the statement that subsequent changes to KARBYTES “always occur within a decade of each other.” The following paragraph explains how to read that statement. If more than ten Earth years have elapsed since the last update, the collection may be assumed permanently closed to further updates under the decree recorded on this page.

The second sentence makes the decade a condition of continuation. A change that belongs to the ongoing publication would follow the preceding change within that interval. If the interval runs longer, the rule gives a later reader a basis for treating the last update as the endpoint of the collection. The clock is tied to the last update to KARBYTES, so the creation or modification date of this particular policy page is a separate matter. The page states the criterion for interpreting the history. The history of the collection would supply the date from which to apply it.

The rule says “more than ten Earth years.” That is slightly different at the exact boundary from the July 2025 wording in KARBYTES_CODE_POSSIBLY_STOPS_GROWING, which says “ten years or longer.” The April wording places the stated condition beyond the ten-year mark. The earlier wording includes the mark itself. The link attached to the word “ten” leads directly back to that earlier policy, making both formulations available. A separate link on “herein” points back to this rule page, identifying the document that records the decree.

CMR1 and the authority to update

CMR1 says that exactly one person is authorized by KARBYTES to make any changes to KARBYTES. Its explanatory paragraph names that person as Kar Beringer and identifies Kar Beringer with lowercase karbytes as defined by the collection. It then expresses the authority in personal terms. Karbytes authorizes only karbytes to update KARBYTES. The name in this paragraph leads back to karbytes_unique_identity.

The change from the July 2025 formulation is meaningful. The earlier protocol says it is generally assumed that only the author implements changes. The April page gives that position a numbered rule and states it as an exclusive authorization. The footer continues to identify the material as PUBLIC_DOMAIN. The two statements concern different actions. CMR1 governs authority to revise the identified original collection. The publication designation concerns the material as released. KARBYTES_CODE_CONTINUES makes the author’s intended relationship explicit by reserving edits to KARBYTES for its author while allowing people to make copies under the project’s stated public-domain rules.

Together, CMR0 and CMR1 provide the two facts a future reader would need to consider when interpreting an apparent continuation. The timing has to be understood in relation to the most recent update, and the act has to be understood in relation to the person authorized to make it. The page records those conditions in prose. Its cross-references give readers places to learn more about the collection and its author.

How the linked documents extend the rules

KARBYTES_RULES_FOR_ITSELF and KARBYTES_CONTENT_UPDATING_RULES point to each other. Each describes itself as extending the other. The first is written in the first person. It says that karbytes speaks on its own behalf, treats its propositions as assumptions rather than expressions of absolute certainty, and takes satisfaction to concern whether a self-aware agent experiences its time as serving its authentic goals. The updating page shifts the focus to acts performed on the published collection. The reciprocal references let the reader move between the author’s account of speaking for itself and the collection’s account of who may speak through a revision.

The link on “Content Management Rule” points to a_karbytes_content_management_policy, which addresses editing at the level of individual pages and micro-directories. That policy describes directing most new edits toward current and future packs, allowing some corrections or essential additions to older material, and treating a numbered extension pack as a time capsule once it has been written to an M-DISC or equivalent read-only medium. Those are decisions about particular parts of the archive. CMR0 sets a different boundary for the collection as a whole. A pack can reach its final form while new packs and their indexes continue to appear.

At the end of the reproduced journal, an italic note points forward to EXPANDING_KARBYTES_BEST_PRACTICES. That page describes itself in turn as an extension of the updating rules. Its nine steps, numbered zero through eight, turn an expansion into a sequence of maintenance tasks. They cover starting a new extension pack from a template, updating KARBYTES_CODE_CONTINUES and the macro directory, checking the portfolio, blog apps, and darknet directory, revising repository ZIP inventories, updating timestamps and checksums, and saving the result to M-DISCs and the Wayback Machine. It calls for at least three M-DISC copies of the relevant version and gives a goal of saving each file to the Wayback Machine at least one hundred times over karbytes’ lifespan. Those are the guide’s stated practices and goals. The two rules on the main page establish the time and authority within which that work takes place.

Publication metadata and EXIF

The visible footer gives April 5, 2026 as the last update. WordPress records publication on April 5 at 05:29:57 UTC, equivalent to April 4 at 10:29:57 p.m. Pacific daylight time. It records modification on April 6 at 05:18:32 UTC, equivalent to April 5 at 10:18:32 p.m. Pacific daylight time. The journal filename, its internal date, the visible footer, and the publishing system’s timestamps are distinct date records. Together they place the page and its source in the April 4 to April 5 period in Pacific local time.

The article body contains text and hyperlinks, with no article-specific image carrying EXIF. I checked the shared site images associated with the page. The header logo is a 160 by 160 pixel RGB PNG of 42,748 bytes. It shows an elongated yellow-gold spiral, with dark inner rings, against a pale mint and cyan field. The social-preview copy is a 160 by 160 pixel indexed-color PNG of 18,333 bytes. The author avatar is a 50 by 50 pixel indexed-color PNG of 3,118 bytes. None contains EXIF or an embedded creation timestamp. The publication data comes from the page text and HTML metadata.

First-person commentary

I notice the difference that numbering makes here. In the July protocol, the ten-year rule emerges from a paragraph about wanting the collection to continue and accepting that it may eventually stop. In this page, I can point to CMR0. I can refer to it while reading another document, or compare its wording with a later version, without carrying the whole earlier explanation with me. CMR1 gives the same kind of address to the authorship rule. I find that especially fitting in an archive that already uses short labels to help readers locate files and groups of files. Here a label gives me a place to return to inside the policy itself.

The phrase “Earth years” also catches my attention. The rule could have used a bare decade, and I would have understood the ordinary interval. The added word makes me conscious of the time scale on which someone would actually have to observe it. Ten years is brief in a document that contemplates indefinite preservation. It is long in the life of a reader who might come back occasionally and wonder whether another entry is still possible. I can imagine two visits to the same page, years apart, with identical words on the screen and very different expectations about what could follow them.

I read the opening sentence of CMR0 with the paragraph beneath it. “Always” initially sounds like a promise about the frequency of future work. The next paragraph lets me read it as a condition for recognizing that work as part of a continuing publication. That difference matters to how I experience the rule. It leaves time for a long pause. It also gives the pause a point beyond which a reader may understand the old work as complete. The slight difference between “ten years or longer” and “more than ten” becomes interesting precisely because the rest of the policy is so stable. If I ever had reason to apply it at the anniversary itself, I would want to know which wording I was following.

CMR1 makes me think about what I would have to recognize as an update before starting that clock again. A host can supply a more recent timestamp. A saved copy can reappear. A reader can redistribute a file with its old text intact. I could encounter each as a new event in the life of the material. The question under CMR1 would be whether I had encountered a revision made by the authorized author to the collection itself. I find that distinction helpful because the archive is meant to circulate. If every new appearance of an old file counted as an update to the original, readers could keep moving the endpoint simply by continuing to preserve or share it.

The source comparisons give me a small version of that problem. The plain-text journal and the monospace block preserve the same nonblank lines. The published article gives individual words destinations, colors the rule headings blue, and places the text inside the blog. The archived HTML preserves that presentation too. I can recognize the same rule in all three forms. I can also ask different questions of each form. A sentence-level comparison tells me whether the rule changed. A comparison of links tells me whether its references changed. A comparison of files would tell me whether the bytes changed. I wonder which level a future reader would choose when trying to establish the last significant act of editing.

I also find the scale of the expansion guide revealing beside the small rule page. A new extension pack can require changes to an index, a catalogue, a checksum list, a home-page timestamp, and several saved copies. From a reader’s position, one new entry might look like one update. From the position of the person maintaining it, the same addition has to be carried through several records so that it remains findable and recoverable. That makes me appreciate why KARBYTES can contain packs already treated as finished while the collection is still being expanded. The acts of closing a particular pack and closing the whole work happen at different scales.

The sentence that says authority is granted “by KARBYTES” is the one I find most unusual. A body of files can preserve a declaration by the person who made it. Once published, that declaration can be read as something the collection says about its own continuation. The following paragraph brings the sentence back to Kar Beringer, whose decision it records. I can follow the movement in the capitalization alone, from the collection that carries the rule to the person who made it. That seems especially apt for a policy designed to remain readable after the author has stepped away.

I am left thinking about the future reader who encounters the rule through a copy, years after finding the original site has fallen quiet. That reader could still see the source date, the two numbered sentences, and the little link on “herein” that directs attention back to the page making the declaration. The page gives that reader a reason to take the silence seriously and a way to leave its cause open. How much else the reader could recover would depend on the paths still available through the archive, and on whether those paths lead to enough of the record to tell when the silence began.

PUBLIC_DOMAIN

PUBLIC_DOMAIN sets out karbytes’ reasons for making the source code and media of Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog freely available. It begins with a photograph of three nested yellow boxes, then moves from the mechanics of copying a digital file to questions of ownership, authorship, and preservation. Its central example asks what happens when two people hold apparently identical files and make competing claims about their origin. The page ends with a practical specification for hyperlink colors, showing how the two sites identify their own files, distinguish code from media, and mark destinations on other parts of the web.

The page and its opening image

The document appears on karlina_object under the site tagline “Creativity And The Cosmos.” Its uppercase title is repeated in the article, with horizontal rules dividing the major parts. The photograph comes before the definitions. Beneath it, a line labeled image_file gives the raw archive reference in orange lettering on black. The next paragraph is highlighted yellow and names Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog as the two collections covered by karbytes’ declaration. It says that karbytes, also named Kar Beringer, releases the source and media files as public-domain intellectual property. In karbytes’ account, that designation allows anyone to access, possess, modify, and distribute copies.

The photograph, nested_yellow_boxes_03_july_2022.jpg, is nearly square and filled by a drawing of three box-like forms at different scales. The largest occupies most of the paper. Its edges slope in perspective, with a broad yellow floor tapering into the drawing and a top plane running diagonally up toward the right. A second box sits within that outline. A third, much smaller one occupies the lower central area. The three dark back planes and the overlapping yellow edges make the forms read as cavities receding into one another. At the same time, the nearer outlines can look like separate boxes standing inside the larger ones.

The drawing uses saturated lemon and yellow-green, with an olive cast in the more heavily textured areas. The left wall of the largest box is densely marked and uneven in tone. The large floor is brighter, with finer horizontal variations. The right-hand yellow faces form comparatively narrow bands beside the black areas. The black outlines vary slightly in width and straightness, particularly where the longer edges meet. There is a thin greenish halo in places around the yellow outer edge. Those details preserve the appearance of a photographed drawing as well as the geometry it depicts.

I compared the WordPress photograph with the linked GitHub copy. They are byte-for-byte identical. Each contains 460,718 bytes and retains the same embedded camera metadata. That makes the image itself an example of the exact copying the page goes on to describe.

Files, code, media, and property

The first definition treats a file as a named, finite sequence of binary digits. The sequence encodes information and can be stored in and transmitted between computers. Its order matters. If each bit is copied in the same position and the hardware operates correctly, the copy can retain the exact information of the source. The page describes that process as indefinitely repeatable. A file can move between physical substrates while preserving the sequence by which the document identifies it.

The page then separates the collection’s files into code and media. In its terminology, a code file contains a complete, coherent set of instructions in a programming language that a computer can process and execute. The example is factorial.cpp, a public-domain C++ source file dated June 14, 2023. The program takes an integer and demonstrates two ways of computing its factorial, by recursion and by iteration. It reports the calculations to the terminal and writes output to a text file. It illustrates the page’s description of code as a written sequence with an operational role.

“Media file” is the broader residual category. In this scheme it includes plain text, images, audio, video, and other files outside the code category. The example is karlina_object_wordpress_logo_orange_box_drawing_2020.png. It depicts one broad orange cuboid outlined in heavy black on a pale background. Yellow-orange occupies much of the upper and left faces, while the lower plane and right side deepen into red-orange. The internal edges are visible through the form, giving it the appearance of an open or transparent box. The blog’s header logo uses the same pixel image. The archive PNG and the header PNG differ in their encoding and file size, and the header adds a pixel-density field.

Both the code and media definitions are highlighted cyan. The document follows them with a deliberately broad definition of INTELLECTUAL_PROPERTY as a collection containing a natural-number count of files. It defines property as something a person can own and control. This gives the later example a simple vocabulary. A person can create a work, make another instance of it, and decide how a particular copy is held or shared. The page then asks how those actions relate when the information in two copies matches.

The page’s definition of open source

The OPEN_SOURCE section appears in a rounded yellow-green panel. It describes open source as a designation made by the original author and sets out five assumptions. People may make and keep as many modified or unmodified copies as they wish. They may distribute or sell those copies. They may edit them. The page’s model also allows people to claim authorship of copies, including unmodified ones. Finally, it says that once the work has been released to the public as open source, it is treated as open source permanently.

The permission to claim authorship is particularly important to what follows. The page introduces the example as a possible contradiction under its particular definition. It sets the example out as pseudocode, with four steps named STEP_0 through STEP_3, two people designated Person_A and Person_B, and two objects named X and Y. Double-slash comments explain the asserted rights and actions after each step, in the style of explanatory comments in a program.

In STEP_0, Person_A creates an original work, X. Under the example’s starting assumptions, Person_A initially owns it privately and controls who may use or obtain it. In STEP_1, Person_A releases X as PUBLIC_DOMAIN. The example now allows anyone to copy it and to hold a resulting copy as private property. In STEP_2, Person_B makes such a copy and names it Y. The example gives Person_B control over the copy that Person_B holds.

STEP_3 introduces the conflict. Person_B declares Y private and accuses Person_A’s X of having been copied unlawfully from Y. The claims appear plausible on the surface because the copies can look identical. Their sequence of creation runs the other way. X existed first, Person_A created and released it, and Y was made from X afterward. The example maintains that Person_A can still control the copies of X that Person_A holds, while Person_B claims control over the sharing of Y. Karbytes uses the resulting dispute to argue that copyright enforcement can be futile when identical content can be used to support a false account of origin. The page favors releasing original work into the public domain to encourage distribution, continued access, and longevity.

The distinction between the page’s model and ordinary licensing terminology matters to that reading. The Open Source Initiative’s Open Source Definition deals with rights to obtain, redistribute, and modify software. It also allows some conditions concerning how modified versions are distributed or identified. The permission to claim authorship of an unchanged copy is a stipulation of karbytes’ example. The U.S. Copyright Office describes the public domain in terms of copyright status, while lasting availability on the web is part of karbytes’ project-specific definition. Sections 202 and 103(b) of the U.S. Copyright Act also separate ownership of a copy from ownership of copyright and limit copyright in a derivative work to eligible new contributions. Those distinctions help locate the conflict in the pseudocode. Person_B’s possession of Y, Person_A’s creation of X, and any rights in later added material are separate matters.

Public release, preservation, and identity

The page defines PUBLIC_DOMAIN as open-source intellectual property considered ownerless from a specified date and thereafter part of the public World Wide Web. Its application to KARBYTES is immediate. According to the cyan-highlighted declaration, each file is treated as open source and public domain as soon as karbytes publishes it on the public web. The word karbytes appears there with two referents. One is the person who wrote and published the work. The other is the intellectual property encompassing the two websites. Their different visual treatments help the reader follow that shift.

The same declaration describes a preservation practice. Karbytes routinely saves the files to the Wayback Machine during karbytes’ functional lifespan, with the aim of keeping the intended final versions publicly accessible and fixed in their archived form. Publication and preservation do separate jobs in this account. The declaration tells people what they may do with the released material. The repeated saves give the material another place from which it can be recovered. The page states preservation as a goal whose likelihood karbytes tries to increase through that work.

A footnote attached to Kar Beringer’s name adds the question of identification. The name links to a page that points to publicly released genomic material intended to help identify the author. The footnote describes the genome as public-domain intellectual property and considers the possibility of other organisms sharing it. The proposed identifier is therefore composite. It combines the genome and the name with the historical fact claimed for the creator, original proprietorship of Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog. An underlined sentence presumes that exactly one organism meets that combined description and that this organism is also called karbytes.

The paragraph immediately after the footnote qualifies the presumption. It is set against a coral-red highlight, with the strongest part of the caveat underlined. The paragraph raises the possibility of phenomenally identical parallel universes and then widens the question to noumena, as defined elsewhere in the project. It suggests that a pattern distinguishable from other patterns could have infinitely many simultaneous copies, including copies with no phenomenal counterpart. This is a hypothetical extension of the identity question. The page retains the original presumption and puts the reason for doubting its unrestricted scope directly beside it.

These are the passages reproduced in the July 2025 screenshot on karbytes_unique_identity, which we examined earlier. In that screenshot, the underlined presumption ends the visible excerpt. Here the live text carries on into the qualification. The relationship between the two documents makes the change easy to locate. The screenshot preserves the earlier wording as an image, while the current source gives the reader the added paragraph in context.

The hyperlink color conventions

The final section, HYPERLINK_COLORING_CONVENTIONS, applies to both Karlina Object and Karbytes For Life Blog. A cyan-highlighted introduction is followed by six gray, rounded panels numbered zero through five. Each panel displays its own heading in the relevant foreground and background colors. A reader can see a specimen of the convention while reading its description.

Convention zero assigns a black background to a raw file in a public GitHub repository under @karlinarayberinger. The heading uses white on black to show the base background convention. The later panels refine the meaning of black by specifying its text color.

Convention one uses black lettering on bright green for GitHub web pages associated with @karlinarayberinger or @karbytessupporter, with GitHub Pages included among the examples. This is the treatment used when the destination is a page in that hosting environment, such as an account or project page.

Convention two uses black lettering on orange for external web pages outside the two WordPress sites and the primary GitHub account. The page uses this treatment on general references such as computer, law, authorship, and the World Wide Web.

Convention three uses green lettering on black for a raw code file in a public repository under @karlinarayberinger. It extends the same treatment to WordPress pages karbytes regards as more code-oriented than prose-oriented. It also gives a special case for a karbytessupporter Blogspot page that organizes raw GitHub references for archiving. The green-on-black factorial.cpp reference earlier in the document is an example of the raw-code use.

Convention four uses orange lettering on black for a raw media file in the primary account’s public repositories and for WordPress pages karbytes considers more prose-oriented. The nested-boxes photograph and orange-box media example both demonstrate this treatment. The visual distinction between green on black and orange on black is consequently useful inside the project, where both links can lead to files under the same account.

Convention five uses cyan lettering on black for a code file hosted on karbytes’ dark-web server. Here cyan is a foreground link color, distinct from the broad cyan highlighting used to emphasize several paragraphs in the article. The scheme gives hosting location, file type, and the author’s classification of a page a visible place in the presentation.

First-person commentary

I first read the yellow forms as rooms receding into one another. My eye follows the large floor toward the middle box, then follows the smaller floor toward the black center. When I look back at the outline, the boxes seem to come forward again. I can read them as three objects, each with a front edge and a set of walls. I like that the same drawing makes both readings available. The outer shape provides a place to stand visually, while the inner shape repeats enough of its structure to make me reconsider which edge belongs to which box. That turns out to be a good preparation for a page that keeps asking what has been copied and what contains the copy.

The actual files sharpen that question for me. The two nested-boxes JPEGs have different places in the archive and exactly the same bytes. If I open either one, I see the same uneven outline, the same mark in the margin, and the same black center. I can even read the same record of the camera in both. The orange-box PNGs offer a nearby variation. They look identical when decoded, yet the bytes and one piece of metadata differ. In one case I can verify the whole file as a copy. In the other I can verify the visible image as a copy. I am interested in how readily I say “the same” in both situations, and how much I have to specify when the kind of sameness becomes consequential.

That is where I feel the force of the example about X and Y. If someone hands me two exact copies and removes the account of where they came from, inspecting the copies can tell me they match. It cannot give me the direction of the copying by itself. To learn that, I would look for an earlier publication, a record of transfer, a dated source, or some other evidence of how the two arrived. I read Person_B’s accusation as a misuse of resemblance. The matching content is real, and the proposed history is reversed. The legal distinctions between a copy, a work, and a later contribution help clarify what is at stake. I still find the underlying problem of evidence worth thinking about, especially for an archive whose files are designed to travel widely.

The author’s decision to favor distribution makes that question more interesting to me. Karbytes appears willing for a file to circulate well beyond the place where it first appeared, while spending considerable effort recording how it was published and where it can be found. I can imagine a reader who first encounters a copied program or image on an unfamiliar machine, far from the blog. The file may already be useful or recognizable. Discovering the original publication would add a history to that encounter. I see room here for permission to copy, credit for the person who made the work, and care in preserving the evidence that connects a surviving file to its origin. Those things can matter to a reader at different times.

I also pause over the phrase that makes permanent web availability part of the definition. The strongest sense of permanence I can see in the page is in the practice that follows the declaration. A file is put somewhere people can retrieve it, copied to another host, and saved with a date. Those acts give access more than one path. The earlier version’s description of the Wayback Machine calendar makes that especially concrete. I picture looking at two dates on the calendar and discovering that both captures show a page called PUBLIC_DOMAIN, while the text at the bottom of one has something the other lacks. Preservation in that case gives me several stable views of a work that changed. Each view is useful because I can place it in time.

The identity footnote returns me to the problem of provenance at a different scale. I can understand why the page combines a genome and a name with the act of originally publishing two sites. The historical relation helps identify the person the author means. The hypothetical about identical universes tests how far that relation can do the job. If a counterpart has corresponding sites and the same apparent history, I have to decide whether I am talking about the creator in this archive’s history or everyone who could fit an equivalent description. I find the underlined sentence easier to read once the qualification is visible beside it. The page lets me keep using the local identification while asking what would be required to count instances in a much larger frame.

The color conventions give my attention somewhere practical to land after that question. I can see an orange background and anticipate an outside reference. I can see green text on black and expect a source file or a code-oriented page within the project. Orange text on black sends me toward a different sort of material, and cyan on black tells me about a different host. I enjoy that the page teaches the scheme by using it. The link to the orange-box drawing is itself an orange-on-black example of how the drawing is classified. I wonder how much of that information would remain intelligible if someone copied the text alone. The destination might survive in the source, while the color by which I had learned to anticipate it might disappear from my reading experience.

What stays with me is the small blue mark just outside the yellow drawing. It has little to do with the geometry, yet an exact copy preserves it with the same care it preserves the largest black plane. A file carries more than the feature someone happened to care about when publishing it. A future reader may attend to the stray mark, the camera time, the nested shapes, or the explanation printed below. I find myself wondering which of those details will first make a surviving copy recognizable, and which other records will still be available to tell that reader how it got there.

KNOWLEDGE

KNOWLEDGE describes how a physical signal can become data, how data can be organized into information, and how interpreting that information can give rise to a private experience. Karbytes uses the word knowledge for that last event. Information can be encoded in a file and transmitted between agents. In the page’s account, each experience that arises when an agent interprets it belongs to that agent and that occasion. A sunrise photograph opens the page. The same photograph can be retrieved from two places, which gives the reader a concrete example of what can travel before the text turns to what happens in a particular act of looking.

The page and the photograph

The document appears on karlina_object under the tagline “Creativity And The Cosmos.” The uppercase title appears as the page heading and again at the start of the article.
