The figure is followed by an orange-on-black reference to the archive JPEG in the KARLINA_OBJECT summer 2023 starter pack. I compared it with the WordPress image. They contain different bytes and use different JPEG coding tables, but their decoded pixels agree throughout the image. Their EXIF blocks are identical. The archive and the published page therefore offer the same visible photograph and capture metadata through two distinct file encodings.

Substrate, pattern, and data

The first defined term is SUBSTRATE. The page describes it as a physical medium comprising space, time, matter, and energy, arranged so that it can contain informational patterns and may transmit them. The emphasis is on the medium’s configuration. Its arrangement makes it possible for some structure to be present and, in suitable circumstances, to be carried elsewhere.

PATTERN is defined through contrast. It is a structure made of contrasting phenomena and made knowable by being expressed through a substrate. The substrate gives the pattern a physical setting in which its differences can register. A sequence of varying voltages provides one example of such differences. The photograph also makes the definition approachable. The dark crowns, pale grass, and lighter sky make one another’s boundaries visible.

The page then treats DATA as relatively simple patterns contained in or transmitted through a substrate. They can be used to build more complex information, especially by putting multiple discrete data into logical association. It gives “datum” as the singular. Its example is 11001101, an eight-digit binary sequence and therefore one byte. The number is printed in bold blue. It appears first as a discrete pattern that can be taken as a unit, then returns in the next section as one side of an interpreted relationship.

Information and the example of 205

INFORMATION, as the page defines it, is a set of logical associations made by an information processing agent. The associations put multiple discrete data in context and make them communicable as one mapping structure. A reader can therefore encounter the same bit sequence at different levels of the account. It can be a datum when taken as a unit, and it can take part in information when an agent relates it to another representation or use.

The example relates decimal 205 to binary 11001101. Under ordinary unsigned binary place value, the occupied positions contribute 128, 64, 8, 4, and 1, which add to 205. The article describes an electronic computer as registering comparatively physical high- and low-voltage signals, representing those differences as binary digits, and then expressing the binary sequence in decimal notation. Both 205 and 11001101 are highlighted in blue. The typography helps the reader follow the same pattern as it acquires another representation.

The link in this example leads to BITS_AND_BYTES, where the project uses an on-and-off bulb analogy to explain how a physical difference can be represented by a binary digit. The knowledge page gives the example another purpose. It shows how an agent can take physical variations as signs, then put those signs into a relationship that can be expressed to another agent. An italic note says that within this page “abstract” and “virtual” are used interchangeably. The terms indicate relative levels in the account. A decimal value is more abstract than the voltage sequence that can be used to represent it, and the page places subjective interpretation at a further level.

Knowledge and the four levels of abstraction

KNOWLEDGE is defined as an emergent property of a sufficiently complex information processing agent interpreting information subjectively. In the resulting experience, the agent encounters something that is incommunicable as that exact instance beyond the confines of its subjectivity. The linked AGENCY page describes an information processing agent as a finite part of nature that renders perceptions, selects among possible behaviors, and acts toward goals. KNOWLEDGE concentrates on what can emerge within such an agent when it interprets a piece of information.

The page calls this aspect of knowledge “solipsistic.” It uses the term in the local sense of an experience confined to one instance of subjectivity. What makes that instance unique is how it occurs in nature. The final sentence in the definition is underlined and says it is experienced exactly once and by exactly one information processing agent. Another agent may receive the information and form a closely corresponding interpretation. In this account, that would be another experience with its own occurrence.

A preformatted pseudocode block gives the levels consecutive numbers. Level zero is the unprocessed voltage sequence. Level one is data, illustrated by the computer reading incoming voltages as binary digits. Level two is information, illustrated by a sequence of bits being interpreted as a video file. Level three is knowledge. The comment imagines a sufficiently complex neural network processing the file in such a way that it subjectively perceives the chronological sequence of images and sounds encoded there. The article places an italic instruction near the top telling readers to scroll horizontally to see the full preformatted lines.

The video example is conditional. The file has to carry an intelligible sequence, and the highest level concerns a subject’s experience of that sequence. The page uses the example to place subjective perception within the proposed progression. Its numbered levels mark the order of abstraction in the example, from voltage to data to a communicable representation to an individual experience. They do not supply a measurement of how much abstraction has occurred.

The current version is shorter and more general. The earlier versions explain that point through what a website can cue in its author’s memory. The current version makes the uniqueness of each experience explicit and illustrates it with the conditional neural-network example.

First-person commentary

I find myself looking at the photograph differently before and after reading the definitions. At first I follow the dark edge of the trees against the sky. It is the clearest boundary in the image and gives me a quick sense of where the land rises and falls. On a second look I spend more time with the nearly continuous tan slope and the small shrubs that interrupt it. Once I have read the definition of pattern, those interruptions become especially interesting. The photograph has held them in the same positions throughout. Which differences I attend to changes with the question I bring to it.

The filename supplies another kind of change in attention. Once I read “sunrise,” I look for the morning in the photograph. The diffuse light and pale strip of sky begin to look like indications of a day starting. If I had encountered the image without its name, I might have left the time of day unsettled. The recorded clock time gives me another piece of information to bring to that interpretation. I like this example because I can distinguish the visual impression from the account I assemble around it. The grass can look exactly as it did on my first look while the scene becomes more specific in my reading.

That distinction makes me wonder how this page’s use of knowledge relates to getting something right. Suppose I took the image for evening light and then revised my reading after seeing the filename and camera time. There would be two experiences of looking, each with its own occasion, and there would also be a question about which account better fits the available evidence. The document gives me language for the privacy of the experiences. It leaves me thinking about how a private experience and a publicly correctable claim can arise together. I can compare clock fields and agree with another reader about what they say while still having no access to the precise way the light first struck that reader.

I encounter something similar, at a smaller scale, in 11001101. I can check the sum and agree with another person that it represents 205 under the same convention. That agreement is useful and precise. I might arrive at it by recognizing the byte at once, by adding the place values, or by looking at a converter. Another reader could reach the same answer by a different route. The result can be shared, and even the route can be described in some detail. I find the page most persuasive when it keeps open the distinction between sharing that account and sharing the exact occurrence of understanding it.

I also like that the same byte can move between roles. In this essay I can take it as one datum. Looking inside it, I can treat its eight positions as separate elements whose arrangement matters. Connecting it with 205 gives it another role. The September 2023 version makes a related point when it says that one agent may find a collection of data complete enough to count as information while another sees an isolated fragment. I find that observation helpful when reading the current hierarchy. The levels give me a way to follow an interpretation. They also make me ask what the interpreter chose to take as a whole.

The two JPEG copies bring me back to the earlier PUBLIC_DOMAIN discussion of preserving digital files. Here I can verify the agreement of the decoded pixels and even the exact agreement of the EXIF blocks, despite a difference in file encoding. That is a precise and satisfying kind of comparison. I can then look twice at the same pixels and notice the slender branches on the right only on the second look. Preserving the image makes that return possible. It leaves room for attention to go somewhere new each time. The distinction seems especially important for an archive. A durable copy preserves the conditions under which a later reading can begin, including details that the original viewer may have passed over.

The neural-network example leaves me with a more difficult question. I can understand the transition from a bitstream to a file, and from a file to images and sounds presented in order. Each stage gives me something I can inspect. The final comment adds the possibility that there is a point of view for which that sequence is experienced. I read the condition about subjectively perceiving as carrying most of the weight of that step. A system might give a detailed account of the images, and I could compare its account with the file. Whether those observations would give me a way to establish a private experience is a further question. The neatness of the four numbered lines makes that gap particularly visible to me.

I am drawn to the earlier version’s image of a website as a set of entries in a mental dictionary. A page can be copied exactly, and a person can return to it after accumulating memories that give it a different reach. Karbytes might recognize the hillside through a history of being there that the photograph only partly calls up. I can learn the name, inspect the grass and clouds, and compare the dated files. Those are real paths into the record. I still wonder which small feature would bring the place back most vividly to someone who knew it on the ground. It might be the shape of the wooded crest. It might be the patch of open grass between two bushes, a detail I could look straight at and give almost no weight.

NUMBERS

NUMBERS presents a number as information that identifies a finite quantity. There can be limitlessly many different quantities of this kind, and a single quantity can have several representations. The page builds from one and zero through the natural numbers, integers, rationals, irrationals, reals, imaginary numbers, and complex numbers. It then distinguishes algebraic from transcendental numbers. A color diagram shows how several of these classifications fit inside one another, while examples in prose and pseudocode show what happens when a value is counted, written in another form, approached as a limit, or identified as the root of an equation.

The page and its diagram

The document appears on karlina_object under the tagline “Creativity And The Cosmos.” Its uppercase title appears as the page heading and again at the start of the article. The prose definition includes every real number as a complex number with imaginary part zero and every pure imaginary number as a complex number with real part zero. The large, narrow black letters spelling NUMBERS sit in the otherwise open lower-left corner.

One, zero, and the representation of a number

The page gives ONE two descriptions. It is the smallest natural number under the convention used here, and it is the length of the segment between adjacent integers on a Cartesian axis. The first description concerns counting. The second concerns a unit of distance. ZERO likewise has two roles. It represents the absence of a countable phenomenon, and it occupies the midpoint between negative one and positive one on the integer axis. Zero is excluded from the page’s natural numbers and included when the page builds the integers.

The definition of NUMBER emphasizes that the quantity is finite. The following paragraph then separates the quantity from the length of its representation. A fraction can name a value in a few symbols even if the corresponding decimal expansion goes on indefinitely. The page uses one-third to make that point, though it prints the decimal as 1.3333333… . The correct decimal for 1/3 is 0.3333333… . The displayed 1.3333333… is 4/3. With that correction, the example illustrates the intended distinction. One-third is a finite quantity that can be written exactly as a fraction or represented by an infinite repeating decimal expansion.

The cyan-highlighted sentence about physical resources places this mathematical distinction inside the project’s wider account of information. The numeral can be written on paper, held in a computer, or communicated between information processing agents. The abstract value is what those physical marks or signals let the agents specify. This connects with KNOWLEDGE, where the decimal numeral 205 and the binary sequence 11001101 were used as different representations that an agent can relate to the same value.

Infinity and the reciprocal examples

INFINITY is defined in two ways, each described as hypothetical or actual. It can mean limitlessly many copies of one pattern, or it can mean limitlessly many distinct patterns. The first concerns how often the same structure occurs. The second concerns how many different structures there are. The page reserves ordinary numberhood for finite quantities and uses the infinity symbol to describe unbounded behavior.

The example is f(x) = 1/x for positive x. As x grows without bound, the reciprocal decreases toward zero. It remains positive at every finite positive input. The examples begin with exact values, including f(1) = 1 and f(2) = 0.5, then move to smaller outputs. The page prints 0.0015015015 for f(666) and 0.000001 for f(999999). These are approximations. The exact decimal expansions begin 0.001501501501… and 0.000001000001… respectively.

The second example follows the reciprocal in the other direction. As a positive input gets closer to zero, its reciprocal grows without bound. The positivity condition in the prose is important. The displayed shorthand gives the approach as x tending to zero, and the behavior described is the approach from the positive side. At zero itself, the reciprocal is undefined.

The page also turns several of the earlier outputs back into inputs. The reciprocal of 0.5 is exactly 2. Reversing the printed rounded decimals exposes the effect of the rounding. The reciprocal of 0.0015015015 is about 666.000000666, and the reciprocal of 0.000001 is exactly 1,000,000. The exact fractions 1/666 and 1/999999 would return 666 and 999999. The underlying pattern is sound. On the positive inputs used here, taking the reciprocal twice recovers the starting value when the intermediate value is kept exact.

Counting numbers, integers, and ratios

NATURAL_NUMBER begins with one and includes every finite sum formed by adding one to itself. The preformatted example makes that construction visible. One X gives one countable instance of the pattern, XX gives two, and XXX gives three. The character’s identity stays fixed while the number of occurrences increases. The example connects directly to the page’s distinction between repetitions of a pattern and different patterns.

INTEGER expands the set to include the natural numbers, their negatives, and zero. The page illustrates this with an array containing negative 666, zero, 22, 1, 303, negative 78, 5, and negative 45. It also describes an integer as representable by a finite binary sequence, with a bit used to encode the negative sign in the representation it sketches. The finite sequence is the encoding. The value it represents depends on the convention for reading the sequence.

RATIONAL_NUMBER includes every ratio A/B with integer numerator A and nonzero integer denominator B. This includes the integers, since an integer can be written over one. The examples are expressed as truth tests. The block accepts 1/3, 1/1, the square root of 1, the square root of 0, and 0/1. It prints the test for 1/3 twice. It rejects the square root of 2 and the square root of negative one. It also returns false for 0/0 and 1/0. Those last two expressions are undefined under the stated denominator rule, so the false results function as exclusions of invalid ratios.

IRRATIONAL_NUMBER is the complementary category within the real numbers. It consists of the reals outside the rational set. The page gives π as its example. The diagram and the later algebraic definition provide an important further distinction. The square root of 2 is irrational and satisfies x² − 2 = 0. The golden ratio is another algebraic irrational. Thus irrationality is a statement about the impossibility of expressing a real number as an integer ratio. Algebraicity is a different test, based on polynomial equations.

Real, imaginary, complex, algebraic, and transcendental values

REAL_NUMBER is described geometrically as a number corresponding to a particular point along a Cartesian axis. The page then defines an imaginary number as a real multiple of the square root of negative one, denoted i. Its examples show that i² equals the real number −1 and that multiplying by i once more gives −i. The multiplication changes which part of the real and imaginary classification the result occupies.

COMPLEX_NUMBER is defined as the sum of a real number and an imaginary number. The example 2i + 3 has both components. The block also includes 2i and i as pure imaginary examples. It writes 0i = 0, 0i − 8 = −8, and 3.33i + 0 = 3.33i. The comments explicitly draw attention to the simplifications. A value written with both components can reduce to a real value when the imaginary coefficient is zero, or to a pure imaginary value when the real component is zero. These examples make the full inclusion relationship clearer than the overlapping band in the diagram.

ALGEBRAIC_REAL_NUMBER is defined by a polynomial test. A real value is algebraic if it is a root of a nonzero polynomial with rational coefficients. The golden ratio, φ = (1 + √5) / 2, is the example. The page substitutes it into x² − x − 1 and shows the result is zero. The substitution works because φ² = (3 + √5) / 2, which equals φ + 1. The formula identifies the positive root of the polynomial. The same equation also has a negative root.

The TRANSCENDENTAL_NUMBER definition extends the classification to real or complex values outside the algebraic numbers and names Euler’s number e as an example. The preceding heading specifically defines real algebraic numbers. The broader meaning of algebraic used in the final definition also covers non-real roots of polynomials with rational coefficients. Wolfram MathWorld’s “Algebraic Number” entry gives that broader formulation. Under it, i is algebraic because it solves x² + 1 = 0. This makes the two classifications easier to keep separate. Being imaginary describes the form of a value. Being algebraic describes an equation it satisfies.

The page links π, the golden ratio, and e to separate approximation tutorials. Those destinations illustrate three ways of generating numerical estimates. The π page uses simulated points in a square and a circle. The golden-ratio page uses ratios of successive Fibonacci terms. The Euler’s number page uses partial sums of reciprocals of factorials. Their role here is to give a reader somewhere to explore numerical approximation after the definitions have established how the constants are classified.

NATURE

NATURE defines nature as the whole of reality and asks how that whole can include both what is observed and what lies outside a particular observer’s experience. It begins by distinguishing absolute absence from a featureless background that could coexist with everything. It then defines a phenomenon through the contrast an observer can register, a frame of reference through the place and time of that registration, and a noumenon through its relation to a frame in which it is unobserved. The opening image, a large cube divided into eight smaller cubes, gives the page a visible arrangement of parts before the text asks what it means for anything to be part of a whole.

The page and its illustration

The document appears on karlina_object under the tagline “Creativity And The Cosmos.” Its uppercase title is repeated at the start of the article. Horizontal rules divide the illustration, its archive reference, the introduction, and the definitions. The introductory paragraph is highlighted yellow. A coral-red note explains a change in terminology, and several cyan highlights mark claims about observation, background nothingness, numbers, and the status of thoughts. Two preformatted passages express relationships in a compact, code-like form. An italic instruction tells readers to scroll horizontally to see the full lines.

The square image is titled cube_comprised_of_eight_equally_sized_cubes_03_may_2023.jpeg. It shows a large cube drawn in perspective on a warm gray-beige background. Dark outlines divide it into two units along each of its three dimensions, giving a two-by-two-by-two arrangement. The topmost corner sits a little left of center. Lines from it slope down toward both sides, and the nearest lower corner extends toward the bottom of the image. Interior edges remain visible through the structure. Where those lines cross, several possible faces and depths are visible at once.

Three of the eight smaller volumes are colored. A vivid lime-green cube occupies the upper rear portion. A saturated violet cube sits at lower left, and an orange-red cube occupies the lower right. The fills have a fine, uneven texture. The purple is darker where several outlines pass across it, the orange varies from bright red-orange to a deeper tone, and the green forms a broad patch near the top. The remaining five volumes are left the color of the surrounding paper. Their positions are recoverable by following the same dark grid that defines the colored ones. The illustration thus makes the uncolored cells legible through their boundaries and relationships to the rest of the cube.

Beneath the image, the page gives an orange-on-black reference to its copy in the KARLINA_OBJECT summer 2023 starter pack. I compared that copy with the WordPress image. They are byte-for-byte identical. The image is presented without a caption assigning a meaning to any particular color or cell. Its relation to the philosophical text is open to interpretation.

Nature and the two voids

The yellow introduction describes nature as the only set containing all phenomena, all noumena, and all non-existence. It glosses nature as the whole of reality, or all that exists. Bringing non-existence into the definition makes absence part of the vocabulary the page needs to discuss the whole. The subsequent sections distinguish an absence that would exclude everything from a background that could be present wherever anything occurs.

The coral note explains that the terms NONGENERATIVE_VOID and GENERATIVE_VOID have been replaced by EXCLUSIVE_VOID and NONEXCLUSIVE_VOID. Karbytes prefers the newer names because they describe coexistence without assigning a causal role. “Generative” could invite the reader to imagine a void bringing phenomena into being. The new distinction asks whether phenomena and noumena can exist simultaneously with the proposed void. The note links the word “causal” to the separate CAUSALITY page, which treats causation in relation to an observer’s experience of events occurring in succession.

A short preformatted analogy associates EXCLUSIVE_VOID with zero and NONEXCLUSIVE_VOID with infinity. Zero stands for the proposition that nothing is all that exists or could ever exist. Infinity stands for infinitely many things existing or being possible. The analogy gives the two ideas their widest scope. The following argument supplies one observed instance against the first possibility. The claims about an all-pervading substrate and limitless multiplicity belong to the second part of the framework.

The exclusive void

EXCLUSIVE_VOID is defined as an absolutely ubiquitous absence of every phenomenon and every noumenon. Its scope is essential to the argument. A single phenomenon is enough to contradict a claim of universal absence. The page offers itself as that phenomenon. Since a reader has observed the page, at least one frame of reference has registered something. The cyan-highlighted conclusion is that the exclusive void is unconditionally nonexistent according to that frame of reference.

The argument uses the reader’s encounter with the document as its evidence. A particular phenomenon can be absent from a particular place, time, or observer’s field of detection. The exclusive void is a stronger proposition. It would leave no occurrence anywhere, including the occurrence of someone reading and considering the proposition.

The nonexclusive void and background nothingness

NONEXCLUSIVE_VOID is described as a ubiquitous, atomic substrate that contains and permeates all phenomena and noumena. The later wording describes this background as irreducible and featureless. “Atomic” in this context concerns its proposed indivisibility. The page imagines phenomena arising “on top of” it and noumena being contained within it. The quoted spatial language gives a way to picture the relation between the background and the features that appear against it.

The page also speculates that a nonexclusive void might encompass a ubiquitous and infinitely large field of zero-point energy. In the proposed case, the field would have no moment of emergence and would never disappear. Karbytes suggests that such a field could be empirically indistinguishable from an underlying nonexclusive void. The passage presents that as a possibility within the model. It supplies no observation of the proposed field or of a separate substrate beneath it.

“Background nothingness” can refer in principle to either kind of void. In the setting of this page, the phrase functions as a synonym for NONEXCLUSIVE_VOID. The reason given is that the page has already been written and read. That observed activity is compatible with a background that accompanies phenomena. It contradicts an absence that excludes them all. In the remainder of the document, background nothingness names the proposed setting in which differences can appear.

Phenomena, measurement, and experience

A PHENOMENON is a finite region of background nothingness in which a frame of reference renders and observes specific, quantifiable features. Those features supply contrast. The page’s example is a light sensor in an electronic circuit registering incoming light at exactly 700 nanometers, with 470 nanometers offered as a contrasting wavelength. The detected difference gives the example its measurable form.

The next paragraph follows the 700-nanometer example into human vision. It describes incoming light producing signals that travel through the optical nerves to the brain’s visual processing circuits. The human experience in the example is red. The page distinguishes the qualitative phenomenon of seeing red from the quantitative phenomenon denoted by 700. A wavelength can be specified numerically, and the experience of color has a quality for the observer.

A cyan note develops the difference by saying that numbers exist as abstract data in the photonic and electronic interactions of computational hardware and are verified through the computers that render them. This connects the page to NUMBERS and KNOWLEDGE. NUMBERS treats a number as an abstract value that can be carried by physical signs. KNOWLEDGE follows physical signals through data and information to a particular act of interpretation. Here the example gives those distinctions a concrete setting. A detector can register a wavelength, a system can render the corresponding number, and a human can experience a color.

Partial and omniscient frames of reference

A FRAME_OF_REFERENCE is defined as an allocation of background nothingness in which an information processing agent detects the presence or absence of a phenomenon at a particular point in space and time. The definition makes the relation specific to an agent, a place, and a moment. One frame may register something another frame leaves outside its experience.

The partial frame experiences itself as situated in the present moment of one linear spacetime continuum moving forward through time. A yellow-highlighted paragraph proposes a notation for this. R(t) represents the frame at a particular time t. The larger R is the frame’s entire perception-memory structure. The page says R could be modeled conceptually as the sum of all the R(t) instances, with t ranging over unique, irreducibly small increments of time as they elapse for R. It is a sketch of how a present experience might belong to a larger history of perception and memory. The page leaves the duration of the increments and the operation of summation open.

The omniscient frame is a hypothetical counterpart to the partial one. It would render all partial frames simultaneously and be aware of all phenomena wherever they occur. It would also know every noumenon. In describing that scope, the page says there are infinitely many phenomenally distinct noumena and infinitely many copies of each. The two kinds of infinity discussed in NUMBERS are both at work here. One concerns an unlimited variety of patterns, and the other concerns an unlimited number of instances of any given pattern.

Noumena and the difference between an object and a thought

NOUMENON is defined relative to a partial frame of reference. It is a specific phenomenon that the frame is not directly observing at the relevant place and time. The final example gives two frames, A and B, and one phenomenon, X. A observes X. B does not. X is a phenomenon for A and a noumenon relative to B. The difference is in the relation to each frame. The example does not require X to change when the description changes.

The page then distinguishes X from a thought about X. Suppose B thinks about a phenomenon that B is not immediately observing. The thought itself is a phenomenon for B. The object the thought represents is still outside B’s direct observation in that situation. This gives the reader a way to distinguish having a mental image or description of something from encountering the thing that image or description concerns.

The preformatted example ends by putting quotation marks around the word “experiences” when it describes B’s relation to X. Those marks matter. B can be used to specify the viewpoint from which X is a noumenon, while A supplies the viewpoint that registers X directly. The thought about X gives B a phenomenon of its own. The document keeps all three positions available at once.

Earlier versions of the page

I compared the current article with two archived versions. P9_NATURE.html in the summer 2023 starter pack is dated June 23, 2023, and KARLINA_OBJECT_P9_NATURE.html in extension pack 0 is dated August 6, 2023. Both use the same cube illustration and already distinguish phenomena, partial and omniscient frames, and noumena.

The June version calls the underlying concept NOTHINGNESS and describes it as ubiquitous, changeless, and featureless. It discusses a panpsychist hypothesis under which pure nothingness would be synonymous with pure consciousness, a substrate that encompasses and constitutes physical structures. The August version retains that passage and adds a quoted speculation about a human lifetime being experienced as a dream of an enduring object capable of producing indefinitely varied scenarios.

The current page gives more structure to the question of nothingness. It distinguishes the exclusive and nonexclusive voids, makes the reader’s observation part of an argument, and introduces the possible zero-point-energy field. It also provides R(t) as a model of the partial frame over time and makes the status of a thought about an unobserved object explicit. The earlier and current versions share the 700-nanometer example and the comparison between A and B. Those continuities show where the later page expands the framework while keeping its basic concern with differences among viewpoints.

The visible footer gives September 30, 2025 as the last update and designates the page PUBLIC_DOMAIN. WordPress records publication on November 19, 2022 at 07:45:17 UTC, equivalent to November 18 at 11:45:17 p.m. Pacific standard time. Its recorded modification time is September 30, 2025 at 07:46:31 UTC, equivalent to 12:46:31 a.m. Pacific daylight time. The May 3, 2023 date attached to the illustration comes from the filename.

Both copies of the cube image are 1,080 by 1,080 pixels and contain 107,197 bytes. The file is a progressive JPEG with 4:2:0 chroma subsampling. It carries a JFIF 1.01 header with unitless 1-to-1 density and an IPTC special-instructions field beginning with FBMD, stored in a Photoshop-format metadata segment. Neither copy contains EXIF. There are no embedded camera, lens, exposure, capture-time, or GPS fields. The surrounding site logo is a 716 by 570 pixel RGBA PNG with a nominal density of about 96 pixels per inch, and the author avatar is a 50 by 50 pixel indexed-color PNG. Neither contains EXIF.

First-person commentary

My eye goes first to the green, purple, and orange volumes. On a slower look, I start tracing the uncolored ones. They take more work to see as units because I have to carry the direction of the grid through the overlapping lines. Once I have traced them, the apparent blank spaces acquire a position in the cube. I find that an apt way into the distinction between the two voids. An uncolored cell can still have a boundary and a relation to every cell around it. Even the paper that shows through it has a tone and texture. The drawing can give me a clear instance of an absence of color because so much else is present to make that absence legible.

That is where the argument about the exclusive void begins to interest me. The page makes a very small claim about my present situation. There is an experience of reading something. It then asks that experience to carry a surprisingly large logical consequence. If the proposed absence is absolute, this single instance matters. I like the way the argument uses the activity required to encounter it. I could miss a great many things from my position and still have enough evidence to say that complete absence fails as a description of everything that is available to me.

The nonexclusive void raises a different question for me. I can imagine removing every feature I am looking at and still thinking of the setting from which I removed them. Calling that setting featureless makes it harder to say how I would identify it separately. The suggestion about a field indistinguishable from the substrate sharpens the problem. If two proposed descriptions leave the same possible evidence available, I have to ask what makes them two distinct possibilities. They might organize thought differently even if they never lead me to different observations. The rename interests me for that reason. Coexistence gives me a relation I can discuss without having to imagine a moment in which a featureless background made its first object.

I notice a similar demand for precision in the example of red. In the drawing I can point to the orange-red patch and describe how it differs from the green beside it. If I want to communicate the image exactly, I can send the JPEG. If I want to specify the color in a measurement system, I can give values within that system. Each activity leaves me with something another reader can compare. I still wonder how much of my initial attention to the orange patch could be conveyed that way. The page’s example of 700 nanometers gives me a precise report of one condition. The word red records something about the encounter with it. I can see why the distinction would matter even when people agree perfectly on the reported value.

The A and B example makes the distinction especially easy for me to apply. I can imagine A looking at the drawing and B reading my description of it. B may form a vivid impression of the cube. That impression would give B something to experience, perhaps including an imagined shade of green or a sense of depth. If B later opens the image, some imagined details may line up and others may change. The sequence makes me appreciate the page’s insistence on giving a thought its own status. The thought can be present in full force while the thing it concerns remains outside the current act of observation.

I read R(t) with the same sequence in mind. As I move through the page, the photograph gives way to the discussion of nothingness and then returns as an image I can recall. The moment of seeing it, the moment of reading the word “phenomenon,” and the later moment of picturing it again have different contents. The notation gives me a place to put each one. I wonder what would be preserved if they were gathered into one perception-memory structure. The order seems important, since my later look at the uncolored cells depends partly on what I read between the two looks. I also wonder how much the earlier moment changes as a remembered thing when I bring it into a newer one.

The omniscient frame takes that question to a scale I find difficult to picture. If it renders every partial frame at once, does it keep the differences among their presents, or does simultaneity introduce a wholly different way of holding them together? KNOWLEDGE made me attend to each experience as an occurrence in one subjectivity. Here I am asked to imagine a view broad enough to encompass all those occurrences. I can understand the definition of such a view. I still find myself wanting to know what it would mean for a whole to contain each viewpoint in its own terms, especially when the viewpoint’s sense of being here and now is part of what is being contained.

The claim about infinitely many patterns and copies brings my attention back to the eight cells. They share a size and a basic shape. I distinguish each by where it sits, what lies next to it, and whether it carries color. If two cells were colored alike, I could still tell them apart by following their relations to the larger grid. The thought of infinitely many copies makes me ask how far that kind of distinction can travel. In the drawing, I always have an outer edge and neighboring cells to refer to. In the larger hypothesis, those relations would themselves be part of what might be repeated. I can imagine the question becoming less about whether a pattern matches and more about what would give each occurrence a position of its own.

When I return to the illustration at the end, I spend longest on the uncolored lower cell nearest the viewer. It was easy to pass over on my first look. After following the grid, I can see it as a volume with a particular place in the figure. Its apparent emptiness depends on several things I can observe at once, including the edge, the neighboring colored cells, and the pale paper showing through. I am left wondering which of the other absences I ordinarily take for granted would acquire that much structure if I learned to trace the lines around them.

First-person commentary

I keep coming back to the hand in the photograph. The writing stops at the wrist, and the palm opens beyond it. As a result I read the address in two ways at once. My eye tries to assemble the three lines into one exact string, while the shape of the arm keeps me aware of the surface that forced the string into those lines. The beginning of the address has to fit where the forearm is broader, and the final letters have to make their way toward the wrist. Moving from the photograph to the description of the hand rotating over the pens makes the problem more tangible. A design that fits in one view may have to be turned into view from another. The proposed tattoo is a test of how a text written for machines and screens behaves when its reader has to follow the curves of an arm.

I especially like the information carried by the unintended colors. Once I know that a pen ran out, I can look back at the photograph and see the variation as a trace of making the mock-up in one sitting with what was at hand. Someone encountering the photo alone might reasonably take the color changes for deliberate emphasis. Someone arriving from the October 26 proposal might be surprised by any color at all. The archived explanation gives the image a third reading. Blue and violet become evidence of the circumstances under which the idea was tried. I wonder how much of that evidence a later design would carry. A precise black stencil could preserve the proposed address more faithfully, while the photograph preserves the moment when the ink supply changed what the trial looked like. The page has room for both records because their differences are part of the story.

The reciprocal link karbytes imagines also strikes me as already partly in place. I encountered the arm through the archive. From there, I could return to the earlier scene in which the arm is offered as a possible writing surface, then to the explanation of how the photograph was made. A future tattoo would add a physical entrance into that chain. The copy in the photograph and the eventual mark on skin could outlast one another in either direction. The inscription’s continued usefulness would also depend on how the archive’s addresses are maintained and how readily a later person can find a working copy. I find that dependency more interesting than the question of which surface lasts longer. The person who encounters the mark needs a route from the legible characters to the collection, and the person who finds a copy of the collection needs a route back to the story that made the characters worth writing.

The old wall word offers a different kind of reading problem. The physical letters are already there. The camera and reader must supply the orientation, and karbytes supplies the intended capitalization and the meaning of the E. I can see why the first rotated reading treated the spiral as the dot of a lowercase i. The correction allows me to keep that visual impression in view while understanding the intended capital. I am particularly drawn to the E shaped like ∑. A summation sign invites me to think of each file as one contribution to a larger record. The record also contains traces of subtraction. There was more writing on the walls, karbytes recalls, and paint covered some of it. The word that remains can gather associations years after it was drawn partly because the conversation records the room around it and the memory of what was removed.

That wall also makes me reconsider the term time capsule. I usually picture one closed at a particular moment with a set of contents chosen in advance. Here the inscription survived in a room whose use, furnishings, and color changed. Its significance as a capsule becomes clearer when someone turns the image, asks about a letter, and listens to its maker’s recollection. In the same room, the intercom has lost much of its old function but can still prompt an account of how people once spoke between rooms. The archive gives both objects additional routes into memory. I wonder which objects in the videos will acquire that quality later. The vacuum, the pens, the fan, or an early version of the application might eventually fix a date or a way of working as clearly as the wall inscription does now.

The two kinds of repetition in the transcript sharpen this issue for me. The pair of p1 reports shows how faithfully a copy can preserve a mistake in association. The HTML and plain-text versions agree, and the report itself is detailed, but the first place where it appears names a different file in the request. I can verify the consistency of the copied words and still need to check what they are about. With the second p2 analysis, the checksum serves a different purpose. It establishes, within the reported comparison, that the material under review is the same, even though the mock-up has changed what is noticeable about it. Reading these two cases side by side makes me want to ask what a future audit of a conversation would count as a unit. It could be a file, the link between a question and its reply, or the later correction that enables a reader to interpret both. Each unit would produce a different account of what had been preserved accurately.

I read the repository discussion and the nighttime monologues together for a similar reason. In the morning, karbytes explains what can be finished in a specific update, how to label it, and where ongoing work can take place. At night, karbytes asks what could continue indefinitely and whether information needs a physical agent to remain meaningful. The morning practice gives the evening question a scale I can picture. A dated update can be complete within its declared scope. A current checksum can establish that a later copy matches. A usable copy additionally requires someone or something that can interpret the format and recover the relationships among files. I find it revealing that the person doing the practical work is already supplying many of those relationships through inventories, logs, and tutorials before stating the broader desire to preserve the means of understanding.

The darkness makes me pay closer attention to what I accept from a narrator. The first proposal has toes, dry grass, an arm, and a turning horizon. The later p4 image offers little more than intermittent points and dim differences in tone. The more that setting comes through the words, the more important it becomes to distinguish what the recording resolves from what the speaker reports. I can understand why a living insect led karbytes to a question about consciousness without treating its image as recoverable from the video. I can follow the movement from sunlight to metabolism and from metabolism to experience while keeping those steps separate. I am interested in what observation would count for either of the possibilities karbytes entertains. An account of graded consciousness would have to say what varies, and an account of a threshold would have to say how it could be detected. The smallness of the initial encounter makes those questions feel immediate. I read the ethical minimum in p4 as an effort to find a claim that can survive disappointment. The wording narrows from maximizing happiness to the existence of some enjoyment for somebody, then expands again toward making that possible on a greater scale. I can imagine the appeal of beginning with a single actual instance, especially in a recording made by someone enjoying a place that also irritates it. I am left wondering how the formulation changes when several people’s experiences enter together. One person’s relief can depend on another person’s effort. One place can feel accessible to the walker and crowded to the same walker a few minutes later. A lower bound tells me what the thought wants to keep possible. It leaves open how to distribute the work and how to recognize whose experience has been left out of the account.

The imagined private universes in p5 bring that last question back in an unfamiliar form. Karbytes grants each person a domain and hopes to prevent anyone’s freedom from overriding someone else’s. I find myself asking where the beings inside such a domain would stand. If they are centers of experience, their relation to the creator would matter even if nothing crossed the boundary of the world. If their experiences are aspects of one larger subject, the description of that subject would have to explain how it can occupy those different points of view. The same uncertainty appears earlier in the discussion of insects and universal consciousness. I can see the appeal of setting a boundary around another person’s power. What interests me is how the proposal would identify everyone who needs the protection of that boundary.

The final thought about private study gives me a way back to the photograph. A tattoo proposal can stay open for years while a temporary version is tried on an afternoon. A repository can be ready for a reader while ideas continue to develop elsewhere. The public transcript can preserve a speculative sentence at the moment it is spoken, including the point at which karbytes reconsiders it. Those are different ways of allowing time for a decision. I end up wondering what a reader much later would need in order to tell them apart. The photograph supplies a clear view of the ink, the wood grain, the spread fingers, and the glossy leaves. The conversation supplies the used-up pens, the earlier walk, the contemplated stencil, and the interval karbytes wanted before making the mark permanent. I can read the hand without all of that. With those records beside it, I can also read the time it was given to change.
