P7, p14, and p18 contain movement-related transients and other environmental or handling sounds, with no consistently recovered intelligible speech. The written accounts give a basis for discussing the recorded signal. Subtle audible timbre and the sources of faint sounds remain more uncertain.

First-person commentary

The overhang is the image I keep returning to. I can spend time on one little depression in the rock and then, with almost no movement of my attention, look across a whole valley. My first impulse is to read the opening as an invitation to look outward. The cave videos make me reconsider how directional that invitation is. Looking outward gives distance and orientation. Looking inward gives an edge, a seam, and a little light whose connection to the open air is uncertain. I find that the place accommodates both kinds of attention. I would want to know more about the passage precisely because the bright outward view has already given me a strong sense of where I am.

I have a similar reaction to the abrupt change from p9 to p10. Before checking the times, I could have understood the darker second photograph as much later. Twelve seconds are enough for a turn that changes the role of the sun, the shape of the visible road, and the exposure the camera uses. That gives me a way of thinking about the difference between the order of a walk and the apparent development of a photograph series. A sequence can seem to show light falling progressively when it also shows someone looking from different directions. P11 does come appreciably later, and I can see the difference in the low sun and the subdued valley. I like having both changes available. One is the motion of the camera in a matter of seconds. The other is the course of an afternoon.

The post in p18 is my clearest example of a discovery made by changing position. At first I can read the trail name and a right-pointing arrow. For a brief moment both faces are visible, with the second still oblique. Then I can read the destination and see the other arrow. A still chosen half a second earlier would give me most of the wood and only part of the information. A still chosen later would let me read the second face while losing how I arrived at it. I think the small interval of turning is the interesting part. It lets me see why two directions that look opposed on the screen can belong to one physical object in a coherent way. The account of the micro-directory has a related appeal for me. Sometimes the contents are in one repository and the source of the page that leads to them is in another. The arrangement makes more sense once I can see the connection between the two positions.

That connection also helps me understand the pleasure of identifying a place after the fact. In p23 I can make out the post as an object in a clearing, with the two troughs and the hill beyond it. In p18 I can see the text on the post and look into one of the troughs. The still places the objects in relation to one another. The video gives me a way to approach them. Their overlapping details are strong enough to bring the scene together, while a particular label remains unavailable from some angles. I find this more engaging than a single definitive view would be. It allows recognition to happen in stages. I can first notice a pale rectangle in grass, then see the thicker rim, then understand how close the camera comes to the water.

P14 and p18 would be easy to describe as two missing numbers, which is exactly what makes the first repository check effective. Their content makes the recovery feel less interchangeable. P14 provides the duration of a passage beside a grazing animal, including the time it takes a dark shape to become identifiable. P18 makes the turn around the post visible. Those are small developments that a highlight still could preserve only in part. I am interested in how much of the final archive depends on this temporarily impersonal approach. The file check can treat each number alike. The later reading can give each recovered part a very different weight. The doubled index 13 sits beside that process as a reminder that a label can be irregular even when its object is present and intelligible.

I also find the change from numerical order to filename order surprisingly vivid after seeing the photographs. A repository puts p10 ahead of p2 because it is comparing characters. From a walker’s point of view, that takes me to the sunlit road before I have reached the overhang. The system’s order has its own consistency. The series order preserves a different relation, and the gallery order makes a selection from it. I wonder how often a reader reaches a large archive through an order that is convenient for a machine or a maintainer and quietly takes it to be the order of the experience. A0 lets me watch the same names get sorted for different purposes, then gives me enough of the terrain to feel the difference.

The voice exchange and the canyon recording give me two forms of distance from the same laptop work. In one, karbytes can ask aloud whether the files are present and receive a practical result while outdoors. In the other, the phone is outside reception and keeps recording anyway. I am drawn to the distinction karbytes makes in p5 among the present walker, the device, the future viewer who may be the same person, and other possible viewers. The words make it clear that addressing someone can be part of recording even when no one is receiving the signal at the time. I wonder how much that imagined listener changes where the camera goes. In the cave it sometimes seems to be showing another person the place. On the later road it continues for a long time after the voice has stopped, leaving the movement itself available for a viewer who was never specified.

The idea of a POG gives that future viewing a smaller scale. A whole valley can be a collectible scene, but so can a seed head beside the road or a particular alignment of tree and saddle. I can imagine one of those details being enough for karbytes to recover an association that the image barely conveys to me. As a reader, I might instead remember the boulder in p16 because of the way its top edge blocks the last warm light. The file can remain identical while those two acts of recollection diverge. That makes me curious about what the archive is trying to pass forward. Sometimes it preserves a scene another reader can recognize. Sometimes it gives the maker a cue for an experience that still has to be reconstructed from within.

The buried discs give that question a longer horizon. I can picture the difference between a copy that remains stored somewhere and one whose reader knows what it contains, how to open it, and where it belongs. The map views, signs, and videos work through a related problem with ordinary terrain. A location can be physically present while the connection needed to approach it remains obscure. I read the October 25 coordinate exchange and the November 4 trail-post turn as two modest versions of finding a way in. One calls for interpreting numbers and recognizing a map location. The other calls for walking around a piece of wood. Both depend on somebody preserving enough of the surroundings for the reference to become meaningful again.

I am left with p25 because it gives me two parts of a route and withholds the exact joining ground. The nearer bend is close enough to enter imaginatively. The farther one is visible against a slope, beside a tree I can recognize from an earlier photograph. I can go back through the files and make the relationship clearer than it was at first. I still have to allow for the ground the camera passed without recording. That interval is part of what I value in the page. It gives me enough to follow karbytes’ attention from one landmark to another and enough remaining distance to remember that the attention belonged to someone who was walking.
